Stored only as `protectionBypassHash` (SHA-256 hex, like `CloudToken.tokenHash`: a random 256-bit secret needs no slow hash, unlike the password), with `protectionBypassPrefix` (its first 12 characters, shown so people can tell which token is set), `protectionBypassCreatedAt`, `protectionBypassCreatedById` and `protectionBypassLastUsedAt`. One token per project: generating again replaces it and the old one stops working at once (within 60 s for requests Nginx has cached). The token is returned once, on creation, and can't be read again.
  - **Use:** a request with the header `X-Codeqlick-Bypass: <token>` to any address of the project that is protected for its kind (previews, and with `ALL` production and custom domains) is let through, for every path and method. Header only, never a query parameter or a cookie: URLs end up in logs, history and `Referer`, and no cookie is set, so each request carries the header. It skips only the password: `codeqlick.json` rules still apply. While protection is `OFF` it does nothing.
  - **Separate from the password:** changing the password or the mode (a new `protectionVersion`) does not revoke the token, and revoking the token does not touch the password or any visitor's cookie. Turning protection `OFF` keeps the token (harmless while off, valid again if protection is switched on). Deleting the project deletes it. Moving the project to another team keeps it (it is a column of the project, so it moves with it, 4.13 "Moving a project to another team"; the new team's admins can regenerate it). A suspended project refuses creating it (`403` "This project is suspended", like the project's other writes); revoking is allowed.
  - **Who:** the project's admins (team owners and admins) create, replace and revoke it, from the console, the CLI or an API token (a read-only token is refused like any write, decision 61); every member sees whether one is set, its prefix, who made it, when and when it was last used. Team events `project.protection_bypass_created` (details `{ replaced: boolean }`) and `project.protection_bypass_revoked`; never the token or its hash.
  - **Nginx:** a `map $http_x_codeqlick_bypass $cq_bypass` in the hosting site (`cloud-hosting.conf.template`) holds the visitor's `X-Codeqlick-Bypass` value, empty when the header is absent or longer than 200 characters. The one check (above) passes it to the API as `X-Cq-Bypass` (`proxy_set_header X-Cq-Bypass $cq_bypass;` in `/__cq_check`, whose other request headers are already dropped), and both cache keys gain it: `$host|$cq_access|$cq_bypass` (with rules `$host|$cq_access|$cq_bypass|$cq_deploy|$request_uri`). An empty value changes nothing, so the fast path (neither marker nor `.cq-rules`: `204` from the file checks, no API call) is unchanged, and a request without the header can never be served an answer cached for one with it. Nginx can't hash, so the key holds the token itself; it lives only in the cache's key zone in memory (the cached file's header holds the key too, on the server's disk under `/var/cache/nginx/cq-protect`, readable by root and the Nginx user only). The hosting sites serve static files, so the header never reaches an origin. The `/__codeqlick/` location proxied to the API blanks the visitor's `X-Codeqlick-Bypass` (and the internal `X-Cq-*` headers), so the visitor pages never see it. The sites' access log is the default `combined` format, which logs no request headers; a custom log format must never add `$http_x_codeqlick_bypass` or `$cq_bypass`.
  - **The API:** in `edge`, when the cookie check would answer `401` and `X-Cq-Bypass` is present (at most 200 characters, else ignored), the API hashes it and compares it in constant time (`timingSafeEqual`) with `protectionBypassHash`; a match answers `204` (and then evaluates the deployment's rules as usual). A wrong token is treated as no token: `401`, so the visitor sees the password page. There is no per-IP limit on wrong tokens: a 256-bit random token can't be guessed, and the comparison is a cheap SHA-256 plus a constant-time compare, so a wrong token costs next to nothing. The load is bounded at the edge: Nginx caches each answer for 60 s with the token in the cache key (a repeated wrong value is one API call a minute), and the site's `limit_req` (per host, 50 r/s, burst 100) caps all requests to it, check included; the per-IP `limit_req` of 10 r/m covers only `/__codeqlick/password`, not the check. Nothing per visitor is kept, so a stranger can't lock automation out by sending wrong tokens, and a correct token always passes. `protectionBypassLastUsedAt` is set on a match, throttled: written only when the stored value is older than 60 s, so a busy test run is one write a minute. Generating a new token resets `protectionBypassLastUsedAt`; revoking when none is set answers `204` and records no event.
  - **Console:** see Console below. **CLI:** see CLI below.
  - **Logging:** the API's request logger redacts the `x-cq-bypass`, `x-cq-protection-key` and `x-cq-access` request headers, so none of them reaches the logs. **Docs:** the `password-protection` page of `codeqlick-docs` in English and Spanish (4.13 "Public documentation") has a "Bypass for automation" section with the header, `codeqlick protect bypass`, a Playwright example and a `curl -H` example, and says that monitors don't send the token; the CLI page lists the command.
- **Not automatic:** monitors and preview checks (`previewChecks`, decision 69) and the website check don't send it: they still see the password page, as any visitor would. A person who wants them to pass pastes the token into a tool that sends custom headers.
- **What it doesn't change:** forms, analytics, the key-value store's public reads and status pages are on the API's or the console's host, not the hosting site, so they work as before (a form's `Origin` is still the project's address). Monitors and the website check (`codeqlick audit`) of a protected address see the password page, as any visitor would (they don't send the bypass token either).
- **Console:** the project's **Settings** tab gets a "Password protection" card: the mode (off, previews, everything), the password (write-only: empty keeps the current one; required when turning it on), the access days (1, 7, 30) and Save; ADMIN only, members see the state. Protected projects show a lock next to their addresses. The same card gets a "Bypass for automation" row: the state (`cqb_xxxxxxxx…`, created by and when, last used or "never used", or "No token"), **Generate** (**Regenerate** when one exists, after a confirmation that the old token stops working) which shows the token once in a box with a copy button and the header to send (`X-Codeqlick-Bypass: <token>`) plus a Playwright and a `curl` example, and **Revoke**. ADMIN only; members see the state without the buttons. Disabled with a note while the project is suspended.
- **CLI** (`codeqlick` 0.5; the project linked in the folder, or `--project`): `codeqlick protect` prints the state; `codeqlick protect previews|all [--days 1|7|30]` asks for the password without echo in a terminal, twice (or reads standard input when it isn't a terminal, like `env add`); an empty answer keeps the current password when there is one (to change only the mode or the days). `codeqlick protect off`. `--json` prints the API's answer. `codeqlick protect` also prints whether a bypass token is set (its prefix and when it was last used). `codeqlick protect bypass` creates or replaces the project's bypass token and prints only the token on standard output (everything else goes to standard error), so it pipes into a CI secret (`codeqlick protect bypass | gh secret set CQ_BYPASS`); `codeqlick protect bypass --revoke` removes it; with `--json`, `{ token, prefix, createdAt }` (or `{}` for `--revoke`). Ships with the first CLI release after 0.5.0 (tag `cli-v<version>`). `codeqlick deploy` and `builds` print, on standard error, that the address asks for the password, so `--url-only` stays one line.
- **Privacy:** no IP is kept (the password tries' limits live in memory); the cookie holds no personal data and is set only after the visitor enters the password, so it is strictly necessary and needs no consent banner.

#### Redirects, rewrites and headers (`codeqlick.json`)

Owner's request (2026-11-13; Notion CQP-148): a deployment declares its redirects, rewrites and response headers in a `codeqlick.json`, as on Vercel or Netlify, without touching Nginx. Decision 52.

- **One file, two uses.** `codeqlick.json` is already the CLI's project link in the project's folder (`{ team, project, dir }`, section 4.13 CLI). The same file gains the keys below; the link keys stay and the server ignores them (as `$schema`). Unknown keys are refused, so a typo (`redirect`) is an error, not a silent no-op.

```json
{
  "$schema": "https://api.codeqlick.com/v1/cloud/schema/codeqlick.json",
  "team": "acme", "project": "web", "dir": "dist",
  "cleanUrls": true,
  "trailingSlash": false,
  "redirects": [{ "source": "/old/:slug", "destination": "/blog/:slug", "status": 301 }],
  "rewrites": [{ "source": "/app/*", "destination": "/app/index.html" }],
  "headers": [{ "source": "/*", "headers": { "Content-Security-Policy": "default-src 'self'" } }]
}
```

- **Where the server reads it.** The deployment upload (`POST /cloud/projects/:id/deployments`) takes an optional multipart field `config`: the file's JSON text. `codeqlick deploy` sends the `codeqlick.json` of the folder it runs in (not of `dir`); the build agent sends the one in the build's root folder (the project's `rootDir`, the source, not the output). When `config` isn't sent, a `codeqlick.json` at the root of the bundle is used (so a file copied into the output folder by a framework's `public/` works). `config` wins when both exist. The unpacked `codeqlick.json` is always deleted from the deployment's folder, so `/codeqlick.json` answers `404` (neither the link keys nor the rules are public). A file with none of the rule keys stores nothing (`config` null).
- **`$schema`.** `GET /v1/cloud/schema/codeqlick.json` (public, `application/schema+json`, CORS for any origin, cached a day) is the JSON Schema of the file, made from the same Zod schema the API validates with (`z.toJSONSchema`), so editors autocomplete it. It carries the hard maximums, not the plan's limits.
- **The CLI keeps the file whole.** `writeProjectFile` (`codeqlick link`, and the prompt of `deploy`) reads the file, sets only `team`, `project` and `dir`, and writes everything else back as it was (including `$schema`, the order of keys and unknown keys), so linking never drops rules. A file that isn't valid JSON is never overwritten: the CLI stops and says so.

**Rules.**

| Key | Value |
|---|---|
| `cleanUrls` | `true` (default): `/about` serves `about.html`, as today. `false`: only exact files and `<folder>/index.html`; `/about` doesn't serve `about.html`. The `.html` address keeps answering either way |
| `trailingSlash` | unset (default): `/about` and `/about/` both answer as today, and a folder asked for without its slash (only `<path>/index.html` matches) gets a `301` to `<path>/`, query kept, as Nginx does without rules. `true`: a path without a file extension in its last segment gets a `308` to the same path with a slash (`/about` → `/about/`). `false`: the reverse (`/about/` → `/about`). Never applied to `/`, to paths whose last segment has an extension, or to paths no file or rule answers. The query is kept |
| `redirects` | `[{ source, destination, status? }]`; `status` 301, 302, 307 or 308 (default 308). `destination`: a path, or an absolute `https://` or `http://` URL. The request's query string is kept unless the destination has its own |
| `rewrites` | `[{ source, destination }]`: serves another file of the deployment and leaves the URL as it is. `destination` is a path inside the deployment, with no query and no host: Cloud never proxies or fetches another address. It is resolved to a file (the exact file, then `<destination>/index.html`, then `<destination>.html` when `cleanUrls`), so a destination such as `/app` serves `/app/index.html`; when none exists the rewrite answers nothing (`/.cq-none`) |
| `headers` | `[{ source, headers: { name: value } }]`: headers added to the responses of the paths that match |

- **Patterns** (`source`; compared with the request's decoded path, without query; case-sensitive): start with `/` and contain no `?` or `#` (a source is a path: the query is never matched); segments separated by `/`; `:name` matches one segment (not empty, no `/`); `:name*` and `*` match the rest of the path, possibly empty (`/docs/*` also matches `/docs`; `*` is `:splat`). A destination may use the same `:name` and `:splat`, only those the source defines. At most 5 parameters, 500 characters per source, 2,000 per destination. A destination path starts with a single `/` (`//host` and anything but `/`, `https://` and `http://` is refused). A source starting with `/__codeqlick/` or `/__cq_` is refused (Cloud's own paths). A rule whose source equals its destination is refused (a certain loop).
- **Order** for one request, evaluated by the API (below): (1) the `redirects`, in file order, the **first** match wins, before any file is looked at (like Vercel: a redirect can hide a file); (2) `trailingSlash`; (3) the file system: the exact file, `<path>.html` (when `cleanUrls`), `<path>/index.html`; (4) only when no file matched, the `rewrites` in file order, the first match wins (like Netlify's non-forced rules: a file always beats a rewrite); (5) otherwise the usual fallback (`200.html`, `404.html`). The `headers` apply to every response served from files or rewrites (not to redirects, which carry only `Location`): **every** matching rule adds its headers, in file order, and for the same name the later rule replaces the earlier.
- **Allowed headers.** Nginx can only add headers it knows by name, so the list is fixed; matching is case-insensitive and the name is sent as written here: `Content-Security-Policy`, `Content-Security-Policy-Report-Only`, `Cache-Control`, `Permissions-Policy`, `X-Frame-Options`, `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy`, `Cross-Origin-Embedder-Policy`, `Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy`, `Access-Control-Allow-Origin`, `Access-Control-Allow-Methods`, `Access-Control-Allow-Headers`, `Link`, `X-Robots-Tag` and `Content-Language`. Anything else is refused with the reason: those Cloud sets itself and a deployment can't weaken (`Strict-Transport-Security`, `X-Content-Type-Options`, `Referrer-Policy`), those that would break the response or identify the site (`Set-Cookie`, `Content-Type`, `Content-Length`, `Content-Encoding`, `Location`, `Host`) and every other name ("not an allowed header; allowed: …"). `X-Robots-Tag` is added next to ours, so deployment addresses stay `noindex` whatever the file says. A rule's `Cache-Control` replaces the default one of that path (the year for hashed assets, revalidation for the rest). Values: printable ASCII, no CR or LF, up to 2 KB each; up to 16 KB of values per rule; at most 20 headers per rule.
- **Limits** (`CLOUD_PLANS`: `configRules`, `configHeaderRules`, `configBytes`): free 100 redirects plus rewrites, 30 header rules and a 64 KB file per deployment; PRO 2,000, 200 and 256 KB. A file over its plan's size is refused before it is parsed.
- **Validation.** The API parses the JSON and checks it with Zod when it unpacks the deployment, before keeping anything. An invalid or over-limit file answers `400` naming the exact path of the first problem (`redirects[3].status: must be 301, 302, 307 or 308`; `headers[0].headers.Strict-Transport-Security: not an allowed header`; `redirects: 101 rules, the free plan allows 100`; invalid JSON: `codeqlick.json: invalid JSON at line 4`), with `errors: [{ path, message }]` (up to 20) in the body, and nothing is kept, like every other unpack refusal. A build whose `codeqlick.json` is refused fails with that message as its reason and in its log. Unpacking also refuses any entry whose name begins with `.cq-` (reserved for Cloud's own markers).
- **Storage.** The parsed rules (without the link keys) are kept with the deployment (`CloudDeployment.config`, null when none), and the API writes an empty marker `.cq-rules` in the deployment's folder when the file has any rule or sets `cleanUrls: false` or `trailingSlash`. Uploads never create it (the line above), so it can't be faked. The rules belong to the deployment, so a rollback or a promote needs no work: the production link points at another folder, with its own marker. The hourly cleanup rewrites a marker the database says should exist and the folder lacks.
- **How Nginx applies it** (8.3), without reloading, through the same single `auth_request` as password protection (a location can have only one; decision 52). The internal check location answers `204` at once when neither `CLOUD_ROOT/protect/<slug>.<kind>` nor `$realpath_root/.cq-rules` exists, so sites with neither never reach the API. Otherwise it asks `GET /v1/cloud/edge/check` (bodiless, no visitor headers) with the protection headers (4.13 Password protection) plus `X-Cq-Deployment` (the id, taken from `$realpath_root` by a `map`), `X-Cq-Uri` (the main request's `$uri`, kept in `$cq_uri` by a `set` at server level, because the subrequest's own `$uri` is `/__cq_check`) and `X-Cq-Args` (`$args` the same way). The API decides everything: it checks the password cookie first (`401` as before), then, when the deployment has rules (read from its stored `config`, compiled once and kept in memory: deployments are immutable), evaluates them with its own read-only `stat` of `CLOUD_ROOT/deployments/<id>` (so Nginx sends no file flags). It answers `403` with `X-Cq-Status` and `X-Cq-Location` for a redirect (`Location` is built whole, query kept as above, percent-encoded), or `204` with `X-Cq-Path` and `X-Cq-H-<name>` for each header to add. `X-Cq-Path` is the file to serve: the exact file, `<path>.html` or `<path>/index.html` found by the order above, or the rewrite's destination; when no file answers (a `cleanUrls: false` path, a request for `/.cq-*`, a path nothing matches) it is `/.cq-none`, which no file can match, so the `200.html` and `404.html` fallbacks apply as usual. A deployment without rules gets an empty `X-Cq-Path` and today's behaviour. Nginx copies the answer with `auth_request_set` (`$cq_status`, `$cq_location`, `$cq_path_raw`, one `$cq_h_<name>` per allowed header) and acts in the main request: `error_page 403 = @cq_redirect` returns the redirect (four `if ($cq_status = 30x) { return 30x $cq_location; }` lines; anything else, such as a wrong key, falls to `return 403`), and the content locations use `try_files $cq_path $cq_path.html $cq_path/ /200.html =404`, where `map $cq_path_raw $cq_path { "" $uri; default $cq_path_raw; }` makes an empty answer today's list (`$uri $uri.html $uri/ …`) and a rule answer exact. Headers are added with `add_header <Name> $cq_h_<name> always;` for each name of the allowed list (an empty variable adds nothing). `add_header` isn't inherited by a location that sets its own, so those lines are in a file included at server level and in every location that repeats the site's security headers (the hashed-assets one; its `Cache-Control` default is a `map` that is empty when a rule sets `Cache-Control`). Nginx caches each answer 60 s (`proxy_cache`, key `$host|$cq_access` as before for protection alone; with rules `$host|$cq_access|$cq_deploy|$request_uri`), so each URL costs one API call a minute at most. If the API is down, protected or rules-enabled sites answer `500` for answers not cached (protection already fails closed); other sites are unaffected.
- **Console:** the deployment's page shows "Rules: N redirects, N rewrites, N header rules" (plus `cleanUrls` and `trailingSlash` when set) and the parsed file, read-only; the list shows a small "rules" badge. Nothing is edited in the console: the file travels with the code.
- **CLI** (`codeqlick` 0.5.0): `codeqlick deploy` sends the folder's `codeqlick.json` as `config` and prints the API's validation errors, one per line with their path (exit `1`). `codeqlick config check` validates the local file without deploying: it sends it to `POST /v1/cloud/config/check` 🔑 (the CLI has no dependencies, so no local validation against the schema, and the answer always matches the server's limits for the team) and prints the same errors or "ok: N redirects, N rewrites, N header rules"; `--json` prints the API's answer.
- **Privacy:** nothing new is kept; the API sees the path and query of requests whose answer isn't cached yet (the same as Nginx's access log, not stored by the API).

#### Abuse and moderation

Hosting other people's files makes us responsible for answering complaints (DSA). The Cloud terms forbid phishing, malware, illegal content, content that infringes others' rights, sexual content, spam landing pages and cryptocurrency mining in visitors' browsers. Every hosted page can be reported at `cloud.codeqlick.com/{locale}/report` (the address, a reason, details and the reporter's email; public, 3/min/IP, honeypot): the team inbox gets an email and Admin → Cloud lists the report. An admin **suspends** a project with a reason: its addresses answer "suspended" at once, deploying is refused (`403`), and the team's owners are emailed the reason with the way to reply; lifting it restores production. Admin → Cloud also lists teams and projects with their usage.

#### Privacy

Static hosting stores the files developers upload (theirs, under their responsibility) and Nginx's access log of each visit (IP, address, user agent), kept 14 days on the server for security and abuse, as for every site we host; the console says so and the Cloud terms name Codeqlick as the processor of those logs for the team. Status-page subscribers' addresses are kept for the team as its processor too (Email subscriptions, decision 105). The account export (`/auth/me/export`) adds the person's teams, tokens (names, never the secret) and events they caused. Deleting an account is refused while it is the last owner of a team with other members (transfer it first); otherwise its own teams and their projects are deleted with it.

#### Forms (phase 1b)

A project's static pages post their forms to Codeqlick Cloud, which keeps each submission, emails it and shows it in the console. No backend of their own needed.

- **Form:** `CloudForm` of a project (up to 3 on the free plan): a `name`, a public `key` (`cqf_` + 16 random bytes; in the page's HTML, so it can be replaced), `enabled`, `notify` (emails each submission or not), `spamFilter` (on by default; off means no spam rule runs) and `recipients`: the team members who get them (1–5, by account; never a typed address, so a form can't be used to send email to strangers). Replacing the key stops copies of the old one.
- **Endpoint:** `POST /v1/cloud/forms/:key`, public, `application/x-www-form-urlencoded`, `multipart/form-data` without files, or JSON. Any fields: up to 30, names up to 60 characters, values up to 5,000, 20 KB in all (files are refused with `400`). Fields starting with `_` are instructions, not data: `_redirect` (where to send the visitor afterwards), `_gotcha` (honeypot: a filled one is answered as a success and kept nowhere), `_subject` (the email's subject). An `email` field that is a valid address becomes the email's reply-to.
- **Origins:** a browser post (`Origin` header) is accepted only from the project's addresses: `https://<slug>.<CLOUD_HOSTING_DOMAIN>`, its deployments' `https://<slug>--<id>.<…>`, its branch addresses `https://<slug>--b-<part>.<…>` and its verified domains; others get `403`. Posts without `Origin` (curl, servers) are accepted.
- **Answer:** JSON callers get `{ ok }` (`200`, or `400` with `{ ok: false, error }`); a plain form is sent (`303`) to `_redirect` or back to the page it came from, when either is on the project's addresses, with `?form=ok|error`; otherwise a short "sent" page in Spanish and English. CORS lets the project's origins call it with `fetch`.
- **Limits:** 10 posts a minute per IP (across forms); the team's plan sets submissions a month (free: 1,000, counted per Madrid calendar month; over it posts get `429` and the owners one email that month). Spam doesn't count, and is accepted even when the month's limit is reached.
- **Submissions:** `CloudFormSubmission`: the fields (JSON), the `email` when there is one, the page it came from (`Referer`, origin and path only) and when; no IP is kept. Listed newest first in the console, searchable, deleted one by one, exported as CSV (`GET …/submissions.csv`, UTF-8 with BOM, a column per field seen). The list and the CSV are the inbox: they hold only submissions that are not spam; spam is its own view (`?spam=true`). Also has `spam`, `spamReason` and `contentHash` (see Spam). Kept 180 days, then deleted (`CloudFormsService`, daily).
- **Spam:** with the form's `spamFilter` on, each accepted post is checked with local rules, no third-party service. In this order, the first that matches is the `spamReason`: `markup` (a value has link markup: `[url=`, `[/url]`, `[link=`, or `<a ` followed by `href`, case-insensitive); `links` (more than 3 links in all values together: `http://`, `https://` or `www.`, a `www.` right after `//` not counted again); `field` (a field named `email`, case-insensitive, whose value isn't a valid address, or a field named `name`, `nombre`, `first_name`, `last_name`, `firstname`, `lastname`, `fullname` or `full_name`, case-insensitive, that holds a link or is over 100 characters); `page` (the `Referer` origin and path are on a site that isn't one of the project's addresses, same test as Origins; no `Referer`, the rule doesn't apply); `repeat` (the same content, the SHA-256 hex of the fields as stored with keys sorted and values trimmed, was already received by the form at least 2 times in the last hour, spam or not: the 1st and 2nd identical posts pass, the 3rd and later within the hour are spam). The honeypot is unchanged. A spam submission is kept with `spam = true` and its `spamReason`, but it is not emailed, not sent to the channels and not counted towards the month's limit. Spam is kept 30 days from when it was received, also when marked by hand (`CloudFormsService`, daily) and at most 1,000 per form: past that, new spam is dropped (answered as a success, not stored). The caller always gets the normal success answer, so bots learn nothing. Members (`MEMBER` is enough) can mark a submission **Not spam** (`spam = false`, `spamReason` null; if it was flagged automatically it is delivered now, emailed when the form has `notify` and sent to the channels, as a new submission but with the default subject since `_subject` isn't kept, unless the form is paused or the project suspended or deleted; it counts in the month it was received; two clicks at once deliver it once) or **Spam** (`spam = true`, `spamReason = 'manual'`; it was already delivered, so moving it back later delivers nothing: a submission is delivered on "Not spam" only when its previous `spamReason` was not `manual`). Marking records no event; switching the filter is a `form.updated` event. The CSV exports only non-spam; delete works on both. The `CloudForm` view has `spamFilter` and `spam` (the number of spam kept); `submissions` stays the number of non-spam.
- **Emails:** each submission to the recipients in their language: the form and project, the fields as label/value rows, reply-to the visitor's `email`, a link to it in the console.
- **Privacy:** the team decides what its forms ask and is the controller of those data; Codeqlick keeps them as its processor (the Cloud terms say so, version `2026-11-13`).

#### Monitors and status pages (phase 1b)

- **Monitor:** `CloudMonitor` of a team (free plan: 10), optionally tied to one of its projects, with a `name` and one of two kinds:
  - **HTTP:** a URL (`http`/`https`, ports 80 and 443, no credentials) requested every 5 minutes with the website check's safe fetching (section 4.12: every address resolved by us and refused when private, loopback or reserved; at most 5 redirects, each checked): up when it answers below `400` within 15 s. Its certificate's expiry is read at most hourly; under 14 days left warns once per certificate. Optionally a `slowMs` threshold (500 to 14,000 ms, off by default) for slow-response alerts (see Slow responses). Optionally it also checks **text on the page** (UptimeRobot's keyword monitors): `keyword` (1–100 characters, no line breaks) with `keywordMode` `CONTAINS` (the page must contain it) or `NOT_CONTAINS` (it must not). The check reads at most the first 1 MB of the final response's body, decoded as UTF-8, compares case-sensitively and counts as a failed check when it doesn't hold (error "The page doesn't contain «…»" / "The page contains «…»"; a body over 1 MB is cut there, not refused), so the usual two failures in a row and alerts apply. Clearing `keyword` (null) switches the check off; changing it, its mode or the URL resets the failures count. Heartbeats refuse a keyword (`400`).
  - **Heartbeat:** a URL of ours, `https://api.codeqlick.com/v1/cloud/heartbeats/<key>` (`cqh_` + 16 random bytes; `GET` or `POST`, public, 30 a minute per key) that a scheduled job calls when it ends. It has a `period` (1 minute to 7 days) and a `grace` (1 minute to 1 day): late when no call arrived in period + grace since the last one (or since it was created). A call with `?status=fail` (or `POST` with `{"status":"fail"}`) counts as a failure at once.
- **State:** `UNKNOWN` until the first result, `UP`, `DOWN` (HTTP: two failed checks in a row; heartbeat: late or a failure call), `PAUSED` (switched off). Every change of state is a `CloudMonitorEvent` (state, when, the error), the incident history; checks (`CloudMonitorCheck`: ok, status code, response time, error) are kept 90 days.
- **Alerts:** going `DOWN` and coming back `UP` email the monitor's chosen members (team members, 1–10) in their language, with the error and how long it was down; certificate warnings too. Never more than one alert per monitor every 15 minutes in each direction; a down alert held back by that spacing goes out once it has passed if the monitor is still `DOWN` (checked every minute), and a back-up alert goes only for an outage that was announced. A monitor tied to a project can also alert that project's Slack, Discord and webhook channels ("Monitor alerts" in Build notifications, decision 64).
- **Slow responses:** an HTTP monitor with `slowMs` alerts when it answers slowly without being down. Only successful checks count (UP answers with `responseMs`); a check is slow when `responseMs > slowMs`. Per successful check: outside an episode (`slowSince` empty) a slow check adds 1 to `slowCount` and a normal one resets it; at 3 slow in a row the episode starts (`slowSince` = now, `slowCount` = 0) and the **slow** alert goes. In an episode a normal check adds 1 to `slowCount` and a slow one resets it; at 3 normal in a row it ends (`slowSince` empty, `slowCount` = 0) and the **back to normal** alert goes with how many minutes it lasted. A failed check doesn't touch the counters. Going `DOWN`, pausing, changing or clearing `slowMs` and changing the URL end any episode silently and reset `slowCount` (the down alert or the person's own change says enough). The state stays `UP`: no `CloudMonitorEvent`, and status pages, uptime and badges don't change. An episode needs 3 checks to start and 3 to end (every 5 minutes), so two alerts in the same direction are always more than 15 minutes apart: no extra spacing columns. Heartbeats can't have `slowMs` (`400`). Alerts go where down and up go: the monitor's chosen members by email (their language: monitor, URL, last response time, threshold; on back to normal, the duration) and, for a monitor tied to a project, its Slack, Discord and webhook channels with `monitorAlerts`.
- **Engine:** `CloudMonitorsService` (API process, every minute, off in tests): HTTP monitors whose last check is 5 minutes old, 10 at a time; heartbeats past their deadline. An HTTP check never runs twice at once (claimed by setting `lastCheckedAt` first).
- **Response-time stats:** `GET /cloud/monitors/:id` adds `responseStats: { '24h': Stats, '7d': Stats, '30d': Stats }` with `Stats = { checks, median, p95, average } | null`. Only HTTP monitors have them, and only successful checks count (`ok` with `responseMs` not null); failed checks and heartbeats don't. A window with no such check is `null`, and a heartbeat's `responseStats` is `null`. Values are whole milliseconds (rounded). They are computed in PostgreSQL over the monitor's `CloudMonitorCheck` rows in each window, with `percentile_cont(0.5)`, `percentile_cont(0.95)` and `avg` (one query with a `FILTER` clause per window); checks are kept 90 days, so 30 days always fit, and the query uses the index on (`monitorId`, `checkedAt`). Display only: the slow-response alerts are a separate feature.
  - **Console:** the monitor's page (HTTP monitors only) shows three tiles, Median, 95th percentile and Average, with the number of checks and a 24 h / 7 d / 30 d switch (default 24 h). The switch picks between the three windows already in the response; it makes no new request. A `null` window shows "—" and "No response times yet".
- **Status page:** one per team, `cloud.codeqlick.com/{locale}/status/<team>` (indexable when enabled): its `title`, an optional `intro`, and the monitors the team chose in its order, each with a public name (never its URL), current state, uptime over 90 days with a bar per Madrid day (the share of time it was not `DOWN`, from its events; time `UNKNOWN` or `PAUSED` doesn't count) and the incidents of the last 14 days. The overall state is "all systems operational", "partial outage" (some down) or "major outage" (all down). Also `GET /v1/cloud/status/<team>` (public JSON, cached 60 s) and `GET /v1/cloud/status/<team>/badge.svg` (a small badge with the overall state, cached 60 s). A team without an enabled page answers `404`. Status pages on the team's own domain are left for later.
- **Notices:** a team's ADMIN or OWNER posts a notice (`CloudStatusNotice`) on its status page: `kind` `INCIDENT` or `MAINTENANCE`, a `title` (1–120 characters), the affected monitors (`monitorIds`: 1–20 of the team's monitors; the public page names only those on the page, by their public label) and a timeline of updates (`CloudStatusNoticeUpdate`: `state`, `body` of 1–2,000 characters of plain text, no Markdown or HTML, shown with its line breaks, who and when; at most 50 per notice).
  - **Incident:** its states are `INVESTIGATING` → `IDENTIFIED` → `MONITORING` → `RESOLVED`, in any order. Each update sets the notice's state; `RESOLVED` sets `resolvedAt`, and a later update with another state reopens it and clears `resolvedAt`.
  - **Maintenance:** has `startsAt` and `endsAt` (the end after the start, at most 7 days long, the start within the next 90 days; a past start is allowed only at creation, up to 1 hour back, so a team can log one that just began; the end always in the future when it is set). Its phase comes from the time: `SCHEDULED` before `startsAt`, `IN_PROGRESS` until `endsAt`, `COMPLETED` after. An update with state `COMPLETED` ends it early (sets `resolvedAt`); other maintenance updates carry no state (they are a note). The window (title, monitors, times) can be edited until the maintenance is completed (`409` after); once it has started, monitors can be removed but not added, so past downtime can't be hidden.
  - **Limits:** a notice is open while an incident is not resolved or a maintenance is not completed. At most 20 open notices per team (`409`). Closed notices are kept 365 days after closing, then deleted by the daily job of `CloudMonitorsService`; they are also deleted with the team. Deleting a monitor removes it from the notices.
  - **During a maintenance in progress** its monitors show `MAINTENANCE` on the public page and in the JSON instead of their state, and send no down alert (checks and events go on as usual, so the console still shows what happened). A monitor still `DOWN` when the window ends (or is completed early) sends its down alert then, within a minute. A "back up" alert goes only for a down episode whose down alert was sent, so a recovery inside the window is still announced when the outage was, and an outage nobody was told about sends no recovery. The time inside the window doesn't count in the uptime, like `UNKNOWN` and `PAUSED`. The overall state gains `maintenance` ("under maintenance"): no monitor on the page is down and at least one is in maintenance. The badge shows it too.
  - **Public page:** open notices on top (incidents first, then maintenance in progress, then upcoming maintenance with its window), each with its latest update and the full timeline; notices closed in the last 14 days appear in the history next to the automatic incidents. Every change is a `CloudEvent`: `status.notice.create`, `status.notice.update`, `status.notice.delete`.
  - **Console:** the status-page tab has a "Notices" section: the open ones, the past ones (paginated), "New incident", "Schedule maintenance", post an update, edit a maintenance window and delete (ADMIN). Members can read.
- **Email subscriptions** (decision 105; roadmap "Cloud: email subscriptions to a status page"): visitors can get a team's notices by email, as Statuspage and Instatus offer. The team is the **controller** of its subscribers' data; Codeqlick is its **processor** (the Cloud terms say so from version `2026-11-17`, see Terms above).
  - **Setting:** `CloudStatusPage.subscriptions`, default false, changed by an ADMIN or OWNER with the status-page settings (`PUT /cloud/teams/:team/status-page`, `subscriptions`; omitted keeps it). It takes effect only while the page is `enabled`. Off or page disabled: the public page shows no form, sign-up answers `404`, nothing is sent and open dispatches are dropped (no backlog is sent when it is switched on again); subscribers are kept, and confirm and unsubscribe links keep working.
  - **Sign-up is double opt-in, as the newsletter (4.14, decision 67).** `POST /cloud/status/:team/subscribers` takes `{ email, locale, acceptPrivacy: true, website? }` and answers `202` whether or not the address is subscribed. A disposable address is refused (`email: disposable`); a filled honeypot does nothing. A new address is created `PENDING`; a `PENDING` or `UNSUBSCRIBED` one gets a new confirmation token (48 hours) and stays or becomes `PENDING` (an `UNSUBSCRIBED` address is never reactivated without confirming again). The email "Confirm your subscription to <page title>" links to `CLOUD_URL/{locale}/status-updates/confirm?token=…`; at most one per address and team every 10 minutes (`confirmSentAt`) and, so nobody can use Codeqlick to pester one person, at most 5 per address a day over all teams (rows of that email with `confirmSentAt` in the last 24 hours; beyond that nothing is sent and the answer is the same `202`). An `ACTIVE` address gets nothing. The confirm page asks for a click (button and Server Action) so a mail scanner cannot confirm; `POST /cloud/status-subscribers/confirm` sets `ACTIVE` and `confirmedAt` and clears the token. `confirmedAt` and the stored language are the proof of consent. `locale` (`en` or `es`) is the page's language at sign-up and the language of every email.
  - **Anti-abuse:** the honeypot and the 3-second rule of the other public forms (the newsletter and `/contact` use them; Turnstile is only on sign-up and sign-in, so it is not added here); 5 sign-ups a minute per IP and 60 an hour per team (the per-team limit is counted in the API process, `429` past it, so a bot rotating addresses cannot flood one team); the cap below; the per-address limit above. **No IP is stored**: the limiter keeps it in memory only, and no table has a column for it.
  - **Cap:** `CLOUD_PLANS.statusSubscribers` (FREE 500, PRO 5,000) counts the team's `PENDING` and `ACTIVE` rows. At the cap a new address gets `409` ("This page can't take more subscribers"); an address already in the table can still confirm or ask again. The public JSON has `subscribe: true` only while the setting is on and the count is under the cap, so the form hides itself. After a downgrade to a lower cap nobody is removed: existing subscribers keep receiving, new sign-ups wait until the count is under the cap. No usage meter or usage alert is added.
  - **What is sent:** one email per subscriber for each of: a notice posted (an incident or a scheduled maintenance: `NOTICE`), an update posted (`UPDATE`), a notice resolved or a maintenance completed early (`RESOLVED`: an update with state `RESOLVED` or `COMPLETED`), and a maintenance window starting (`MAINTENANCE_STARTED`, at `startsAt`; not when the maintenance was created already in progress, which sends only `NOTICE`, nor when it was completed before it started). **Never** for raw monitor changes (down, up, slow, certificates: notices are the team's own words), for edits of a notice's title, monitors or window, or for deleting a notice. Posting a notice or an update writes its `CloudStatusDispatch` row in the same transaction, only while subscriptions are on and the page is enabled. A later update that reopens an incident is an `UPDATE`. Only subscribers `ACTIVE` with `confirmedAt` before the dispatch was created receive it, so nobody gets news from before they joined.
  - **Email:** plain text and the branded HTML of `email-layout.ts`, built by `cloudStatusEmail` in `cloud-emails.ts`, in the subscriber's language (EN/ES; the notice's title and update text are the team's own words, in whatever language it wrote them, escaped in the HTML). Subject: `[<page title>] <Incident|Scheduled maintenance|Maintenance started|Resolved|Update>: <notice title>` (Spanish: `Incidencia`, `Mantenimiento programado`, `Mantenimiento iniciado`, `Resuelto`, `Actualización`), whitespace collapsed, at most 150 characters. Body: the notice's state (for a maintenance, its window in Madrid time), the update's text with its line breaks (for `MAINTENANCE_STARTED`, the latest update), the affected monitors' public labels, a button "View status page" (`CLOUD_URL/{locale}/status/<team>`), and a note saying why they get it ("You subscribed to updates from <page title>, sent by Codeqlick for <team name>") with the unsubscribe link (`unsubscribeUrl` of `email-layout.ts`: `CLOUD_URL/{locale}/status-updates/unsubscribe?token=…`). Every notice email (not the confirmation) has `List-Unsubscribe: <PUBLIC_API_URL/cloud/status-subscribers/unsubscribe/:token>` and `List-Unsubscribe-Post: List-Unsubscribe=One-Click` (RFC 8058, as decision 70). No reply-to.
  - **Sending** (`CloudStatusSubscribersService`, every minute, off in tests; a run does nothing while the previous one still runs): first, each maintenance whose `startsAt` has passed, is not completed and has `startNotifiedAt` null is claimed by setting `startNotifiedAt` where it is still null (one conditional update, so two API processes make one dispatch) and gets a `MAINTENANCE_STARTED` dispatch while subscriptions are on; a maintenance created already in progress has `startNotifiedAt` set at creation, and moving a maintenance's start into the future clears it. Then the open dispatches, oldest first (at most 3 a run, at most 400 emails a run in all): a batch is claimed **before** sending by moving the dispatch's `cursor` past up to 200 eligible subscribers (ordered by id; one conditional update on the old cursor, so a crash loses a batch rather than sending it twice, as the newsletter) after reserving room in the team's hour (`CloudStatusPage.emailWindowStart` / `emailsInWindow`: a window that starts at the first send and lasts an hour; the batch is cut to what `CLOUD_PLANS.statusEmailsPerHour`, FREE 1,000 and PRO 10,000, leaves, and a dispatch with no room waits for the next window). Emails go one by one with a 100 ms pause; a failed send is logged (never with the address) and skipped, not retried. A dispatch is completed (`completedAt`) when no eligible subscriber is left, and dropped without sending when the page is disabled, subscriptions are off or it is more than 24 hours old (stale news is worse than none); `sent` counts the emails. Deleting a notice deletes its dispatches. Completed dispatches are deleted after 30 days. Subscribers are read again per batch, so someone who unsubscribes midway gets no more.
  - **Unsubscribing** is one step with no session: the link in each email opens `CLOUD_URL/{locale}/status-updates/unsubscribe?token=…` (a button; its Server Action calls `POST /cloud/status-subscribers/unsubscribe`, so a link scanner's GET changes nothing); the one-click `POST /cloud/status-subscribers/unsubscribe/:token` does the same; `GET` on that address redirects (302) to the page in the subscriber's language (`en` for an unknown token). It sets `UNSUBSCRIBED` and `unsubscribedAt`, clears any confirmation token, is idempotent and keeps the row so a later sign-up is told apart.
  - **Retention:** daily, `PENDING` rows whose `confirmExpiresAt` is more than 7 days past are deleted (about 9 days after sign-up), and so are `UNSUBSCRIBED` rows whose `unsubscribedAt` is more than 12 months past (address and date kept only to respect and prove the opt-out). `ACTIVE` rows stay until the person unsubscribes or the team deletes them. Deleting the team deletes its subscribers and dispatches with it (cascade), emailing no one; so does deleting an account that owns the team alone. The account export (`/auth/me/export`) never includes a team's subscribers (they are the team's data, not the member's).
  - **Console:** the status-page tab has an "Email subscriptions" card (ADMIN or OWNER): the on/off switch (in the page's settings form), the counts (active, pending, unsubscribed) and "n of <cap>", a list with a status filter and **masked** addresses (`j***@e***.com`: the first character of the local part and of the domain's first label, and the last label), a delete button per row (erasure; the person can sign up again) and "Delete all subscribers" (with a confirmation), and a note that the team is responsible for these addresses and that Codeqlick processes them on its behalf. Members don't see the card, and the API answers them `403`.
  - **Team events** (`CloudEvent`, section 5 rules): `status_subscribers.enabled` and `status_subscribers.disabled` when the setting changes; `status_subscriber.deleted` with the subscriber's id as `targetLabel` and `details` `{ status }`; `status_subscribers.deleted_all` with `details` `{ count }`. Never the email address, in an event or in any log. People confirming or unsubscribing themselves are not team events.
  - **Public form:** under the page's header, "Get updates by email": an email field, a checkbox ("I agree to receive this page's updates by email"), the honeypot and the 3-second rule; under it a short notice: "<team name> is responsible for your address and Codeqlick processes it on its behalf. Privacy policy. You can unsubscribe at any time from any email." (the link goes to the privacy policy's section on them: `/en/privacy#status-page-subscribers`, `/es/privacy#suscriptores-de-paginas-de-estado`; `content/legal/anchors.ts`). Success only says to check the inbox. The confirm and unsubscribe pages are `cloud.codeqlick.com/{locale}/status-updates/confirm` and `…/unsubscribe` (not indexed), with a link back to the page.
  - **Terms and privacy:** the Cloud terms gain a data-processing clause for the subscribers (Codeqlick as processor, the team as controller: purpose, acting only on the team's instructions given through the settings, confidentiality, security, OVHcloud as sub-processor, deletion when the team deletes them or the team, assistance with the subscribers' rights), so `CLOUD_TERMS_VERSION` becomes `2026-11-17` and every member accepts again (see Terms above). The main privacy policy (`content/legal`, EN and ES) gets a section for status-page subscribers ("Status page subscribers"): who the controller is, what is kept (email, language, status, dates), the purpose, consent as the basis, retention as above, unsubscribing from any email, and Codeqlick as processor for the team.
  - **Unaffected:** moving a project or a monitor to another team (notices and the status page belong to the team); deleting a monitor (it leaves the notices, as above); plan changes other than the cap and the hourly limit above.

#### Website check API (phase 1b)

- `POST /v1/cloud/audits` 🔑 `{ url, locale? }` runs the free website check (section 4.12, the same checks, texts and safety, PageSpeed when `PAGESPEED_API_KEY` is set) for a team's member and answers when it finishes (within 60 s, else `504`): `{ id, url, finalUrl, score, categories: [{ key, name, score, checks: [{ id, status, title, advice }] }], createdAt }`. It is a developer tool: no lead, no email. 20 an hour per team (plan `auditsPerHour`); a URL that can't be opened answers `422` with the reason.
- **Preview checks:** a project can have the same check run on each pull request's preview ("Check each preview", specified under Builds from Git; decision 69). It uses the same code and safety as `POST /v1/cloud/audits`, but no member runs it: the audit is stored with `userId` null and `kind` `PREVIEW`, no `audit.run` team event is recorded (the build is the trail) and the report's language is English. It counts in the team's `auditsPerHour` like any other check. Preview addresses answer `X-Robots-Tag: noindex` (Static hosting), which the check would always fail as "hidden from search engines", so for a preview check the `indexable` check is left out of the Google category and the score.
- `CloudAudit` keeps each one (the team, who, the URL, score and result) 30 days, except scheduled ones (`kind` `SCHEDULED`), which are kept 90 days; `GET /v1/cloud/teams/:team/audits` lists them (each with `kind`: `manual`, `preview` (made for a build) or `scheduled`; `userId` is null for the last two), `GET /v1/cloud/audits/:id` returns one. The console's "Website check" page runs one and shows the recent ones, the preview ones marked "Preview check" and the scheduled ones "Weekly check".
- **Production checks** (decision 81): a project can have its live site checked once a week and its admins told when the score drops. It is a setting of the project, not of Git, because projects deployed with the CLI have a production too.
  - **Setting:** `CloudProject.productionChecks` (default off: every check costs an audit and up to a minute of server time), changed with `PATCH /cloud/projects/:id` (ADMIN). Turning it on sets `productionCheckNextAt` to the project's next slot; turning it off clears it. Team events `project.production_checks_enabled` and `project.production_checks_disabled` (label: the project's slug). Moving the project to another team switches it off (its history stays with the source team, see Moving a project).
  - **Slot:** each project has a fixed weekly slot, computed and never stored, from SHA-256 of the project's id: first byte mod 7 + 1 is the weekday (1–7, Monday first), second byte mod 5 + 1 the hour (01:00–05:59), third byte mod 60 the minute, all Madrid time (decision 65's helpers: `nextRebuild` logic with a weekly schedule). A computed slot needs no column and no "assign" step, and a project never moves unless its id does; only the next run (`productionCheckNextAt`) is stored, because the claim needs it. The spread (2,100 slots) keeps the weekly checks from arriving together, so a team's `auditsPerHour` is not used up by them.
  - **Job:** `CloudProductionChecksService` (in the API, every minute, off in tests; a tick does nothing while the previous one still runs) takes up to 5 projects with `productionChecks` on, not deleted and `productionCheckNextAt` due, oldest first. Each is claimed exactly like a scheduled rebuild: `updateMany` where `id`, `productionCheckNextAt` equals the value read and `productionChecks` is true, setting `productionCheckNextAt` to the next slot after now; no row updated means another process or restart has it. So a run happens once even with several API processes, and an API that was down runs a late check once on boot (not one per missed week).
  - **Address:** always `https://<slug>.<CLOUD_HOSTING_DOMAIN>/`, never a custom domain. It is the address Cloud itself serves and always has a valid certificate, so the weekly numbers compare like with like; a custom domain can change between weeks (added, removed, behind a customer's DNS or CDN) and would make a score move that Cloud didn't cause. The customer's domain is watched by an uptime monitor (certificate warnings included). A follow-up may add "check my domain".
  - **Skipped** (nothing is stored, nothing is sent; a line in the API's log; the slot still moves on): `productionChecks` switched off meanwhile; the project deleted or suspended; no production deployment; `protection` `ALL` (the check would score the password page); the team past its `auditsPerHour` (the same `429` as any check; no retry before next week). A site that can't be opened (`422`) or takes over 60 s (`504`) is also skipped: downtime is the monitors' business, and a failed audit would be noise in the history.
  - **The check:** the same code and safety as `POST /v1/cloud/audits` and the preview checks (`CloudAuditsService.execute`), with every check, `indexable` included (production isn't `noindex`). Stored with `kind` `SCHEDULED`, `projectId` set, `userId` null and the team's `auditsPerHour` counted. No `audit.run` event. The result stores check ids and numbers, not texts, so it has no language: the console and the emails render it in the reader's language.
  - **Comparison:** the previous check is the newest `SCHEDULED` audit of the same project (any age within the 90 days kept; none: nothing to compare, no alert). The new one **dropped** when any of these holds:
    - the overall `score` is 10 or more points lower;
    - a category score (`result.categories`: `speed`, `mobile`, `seo` (shown as "Google") or `security`) is 10 or more points lower;
    - a check of both results (same `id`, from the 27 of section 4.12) goes from `pass` to `fail`. `ttfb` (one measurement), `psi-performance` and `psi-lcp` (Google's lab numbers) vary from run to run, so they count in the scores but never trigger this rule alone. A check missing from one result (the PageSpeed ones without `PAGESPEED_API_KEY`) is not compared.
    The next week compares with this check, not with the last good one, so a steady low score alerts once, when it falls. Recovery is not announced (a non-goal). Nothing records that an alert was sent; whether a check dropped is computed from the two audits, so the history (below) can show it.
  - **Alert:** when it dropped, in the background right after the audit is stored (never failing the job; errors are logged):
    - **Email** to the team's owners and admins (`CloudNotificationsRepository.admins`: active `OWNER` and `ADMIN` members; Cloud has no email preferences yet, so none is applied), in each person's language, plain text plus branded HTML (`cloudProductionCheckEmail` in `cloud-emails.ts`, built with `renderEmail`). Subject `Website score dropped: <project> (92 → 78)`. Body: the address; one line per drop, overall first, then categories, then checks (`Google 95 → 70`, `HSTS: pass → fail`, with the check's title); the date of both checks, a link to the previous report and, as the button, "See the new report" (`<CLOUD_URL>/<locale>/console/<team>/audits/<id>`).
    - **Slack, Discord and webhook channels** of the project with `monitorAlerts` on and enabled (decision 64; email channels never get it), event `audit.dropped`, in the channel's `locale`, sent by `CloudNotificationsService` like monitor alerts (same safe delivery, signing, failure counting and switch-off). Webhook body: `{ event: "audit.dropped", project: { slug, name }, audit: { id, url, score, categories: { speed, mobile, seo, security }, createdAt }, previous: { id, score, categories, createdAt }, dropped: { score: { from, to } | null, categories: [{ key, from, to }], checks: [{ id, category, title }] }, consoleUrl, sentAt }` (`title` in English; `consoleUrl` the new report), with `X-Codeqlick-Event: audit.dropped` and the same signature. Slack and Discord: "📉 *web* · website score dropped from 92 to 78 (Google 95 → 70, 2 checks failed)" with a link to the report; Discord as an orange embed. The channel's `monitorAlerts` switch is now described in the console as "Monitor alerts and website-check drops".
  - **Retention:** the comparison needs last week's audit, and audits are deleted after 30 days, which a week-by-week run would survive but a project paused for a month would not, and the history needs 12 weeks. So `SCHEDULED` audits are kept 90 days (12 weeks is 84 days) and the other kinds stay at 30. The alternative, a score summary on the project, would need a second copy of the result (the check ids) and a way to keep the report linkable; a longer retention for one kind needs a single `where` in the daily cleanup. At one audit a week per project, the extra storage is negligible.
  - **API:** `GET /cloud/projects/:id/production-checks` (any member) answers the setting, the next run, the address and the last 12 scheduled checks with whether each dropped; `PATCH /cloud/projects/:id` `{ productionChecks }` changes the setting (section 5).
  - **Console:** the **Settings** tab (next to Password protection) has a "Weekly website check" card: a checkbox "Check the live site every week and tell the admins when the score drops" (ADMIN; others see it read-only), the next run (the day and hour in Madrid time) and a note that alerts go to the team's admins by email and to the project's channels with monitor alerts. The project's overview (**Deployments** tab) shows, while the setting is on or there is history, a "Website check" card: the last scheduled score with its date and a link to the report, the four category scores, and a list of the last 12 weeks (date and score, with a mark on the weeks that dropped and a link to each report). With no check yet: "The first check runs on <day, hour>". The Website check page lists scheduled checks as "Weekly check".
  - **CLI:** none. The setting isn't part of the Git settings that `codeqlick git` prints (it is not a Git setting), and the weekly check is a console feature; a CI check stays `codeqlick audit`.
  - **Not included:** recovery notices, choosing the day, hour or address, checking several pages, retrying a check that was skipped, a "run now" button (`POST /cloud/audits` already does that on demand), and notices in the status page.
- **In CI:** `codeqlick audit <url> [--min-score 80] [--fail-on fail|warn] [--json]` prints the score and the problems and exits `1` below the minimum or when a check of that level appears, so a GitHub Actions step (`npx @codeqlick/cli audit https://<preview> --min-score 80` with `CODEQLICK_TOKEN`) fails the pull request. `codeqlick deploy` prints the deployment's URL on its own line with `--url-only`, to chain them.

#### Builds from Git (phase 2)

Owner's choice (2026-11-12). A project connected to a GitHub repository is built and deployed on every push, and its pull requests get preview addresses. Decisions 40–42.

- **GitHub App "Codeqlick Cloud"** (one, owned by the Codeqlick organisation, installable by any account): permissions *Contents: read*, *Metadata: read*, *Pull requests: read and write*, *Commit statuses: read and write*; events `push`, `pull_request`, `installation`, `installation_repositories`; webhook `https://api.codeqlick.com/v1/cloud/github/webhook` with a secret; setup URL `https://cloud.codeqlick.com/github/setup` with **Request user authorization (OAuth) during installation** on. Settings in `GITHUB_APP_*` (8.4); without them the Git tab says it isn't set up and nothing else changes.
- **Connecting:** in a project's **Git** tab an admin presses "Connect GitHub" and installs the app on the repositories they choose (`https://github.com/apps/<slug>/installations/new?state=…`; the state is signed, names the team and the person and lasts 30 minutes). GitHub comes back to `/github/setup?installation_id&code&state`; the API exchanges `code` for the person's GitHub token, checks with it that the person can see that installation (`GET /user/installations`), and only then links the installation to the team (`CloudGitInstallation`: its id, the account's login and type). The token isn't kept. An installation belongs to one team. Then the admin picks the repository (the installation's repositories, through an installation token) and the production branch.
- **Build settings** (project, all optional): root folder, install command (`npm ci`), build command (`npm run build`), output folder (`dist`), Node version (`20`, `22` or `24`), build other branches as previews (on), build pull requests (on), **check each preview** (`previewChecks`, off: it costs website checks and time; see Back on GitHub), only build when the root folder changes (off). While the install or build command is left at its default, the build uses pnpm or Yarn instead when the repository does ("Package managers" below).
- **Only when the root folder changes** (`onlyRootChanges`, for monorepos where several projects share a repository; ignored while the root folder is empty): a push or pull request whose changed files are all outside the project's root folder queues no build for that project. A file counts as inside when its path is the folder or starts with `<folder>/` (both names of a renamed file count). For a push the files are those of every commit in the delivery (`added`, `modified`, `removed`); for a pull request, the files of GitHub's comparison between its base and head (`GET /repos/<repo>/compare/<base>...<head>`, through an installation token). When the list can't be trusted the build runs as usual: a new branch, a forced push, a delivery without commits or with 2,048 (GitHub's cap), a commit listing 3,000 files or more (its cap per commit), a push listing no files at all (an empty commit, pushed to force a build, or a merge whose lists GitHub leaves empty), a comparison without a file list or with 300 files or more (its cap), or a comparison GitHub refused. A skipped commit gets no build, no commit status (the `Codeqlick Cloud` status is shared by every project of the repository, so another project's state must not be overwritten) and no pull request comment, like a commit with `[skip cloud]`. "Deploy again" always builds.
- **Suggested settings** (roadmap item proposed by the agent team, 2026-11-14; decision 75): when a repository is chosen, the console and the CLI fill the build settings from what the repository contains, so the first build doesn't fail on the wrong output folder or Node version. Nothing is saved until the person connects; every suggested field can be changed.
  - **What is read** (through a token of the team's installation limited to that repository, at the chosen branch, at most 5 GitHub requests counting the token; the branch follows the same rule as the saved setting): the root folder's listing, its `package.json` (at most 512 KB) and, when present there, `.nvmrc` or `.node-version`, and the Next.js config file. Nothing is kept.
  - **Framework** (the first dependency found in `dependencies` or `devDependencies`, in this order): Astro (`astro`, output `dist`), Docusaurus (`@docusaurus/core`, `build`), Gatsby (`gatsby`, `public`), Next.js (`next`, `out`; a note `next-not-static` when the config file doesn't contain `output: 'export'`, since only a static export can be served), Nuxt (`nuxt`, build `npx nuxi generate`, `.output/public`), SvelteKit (`@sveltejs/kit`, `build`; a note `sveltekit-no-static-adapter` without `@sveltejs/adapter-static`), Eleventy (`@11ty/eleventy`, `_site`), Create React App (`react-scripts`, `build`), Vite (`vite`, `dist`). None of them: `framework: null` with the defaults.
  - **Commands:** the install command stays `npm ci` (builds pick pnpm or Yarn from the lockfile, "Package managers"). The build command is `npx nuxi generate` for Nuxt (its `build` script makes a server, not static pages); for the others `npm run build` when `package.json` has a `build` script, otherwise the framework's own (`npx astro build`, `npx docusaurus build`, `npx gatsby build`, `npx next build`, `npx nuxi generate`, `npx vite build`, `npx @11ty/eleventy`, `npx react-scripts build`, `npx vite build` for SvelteKit), and `npm run build` when there is no framework either.
  - **Node version:** from `.nvmrc` or `.node-version` (a version such as `22`, `v20.11.1` or `lts/iron`, where `iron` is 20, `jod` 22 and `krypton` 24), otherwise `engines.node`. The first major version number decides: a range that starts with `>` or `>=` takes the newest supported (`24`); otherwise the smallest supported version at or above it (`20`, `22` or `24`); a version above 24 takes `24` and one we can't read keeps the default, each with the note `node-unsupported`. Without any of them: `24`.
  - **No `package.json`** in the root folder: the defaults and the note `no-package-json` (the console suggests `codeqlick deploy` for a folder that needs no build, or choosing the right root folder).
  - **API:** `GET /cloud/teams/:team/github/detect?installationId&repoId&branch&rootDir` (team ADMIN, the installation must be the team's; 20 a minute per address, like the other Cloud limits). `404` when GitHub doesn't know the repository, branch or root folder for that installation; `503` without the GitHub App or while GitHub limits us (its 403 or 429).
  - **Console:** the Git tab's connect form asks for the suggestion when the repository, the branch or the root folder changes, but only while the project isn't connected yet or a different repository is chosen (a connected project's saved settings are never replaced by a suggestion for its own repository); it fills the fields the person hasn't edited, and says what it found ("Astro detected in package.json · Node 22 from .nvmrc") with the notes in their language. If the call fails the form stays as it was.
  - **CLI:** `codeqlick git connect <owner/repo>` asks for the suggestion and uses it for every option not given (`--build`, `--output`, `--node`), printing what it found and the notes before connecting, under the same rule: a project already connected to that repository keeps its saved settings for the options not given.
- **Environment variables** (`CloudEnvVar`): up to 50 per project, names `[A-Z_][A-Z0-9_]{0,63}`, values up to 4 KB, each for production, previews or both. **For one branch:** a preview variable can name a Git branch (`branch`, 1–200 characters, no spaces or control characters, as Git allows); it is then preview-only and given only to preview builds of that branch, where it replaces the general variable with the same key. So `API_URL` can exist for all previews and again for `staging`. Production builds never get branch variables. The same key may exist once per branch plus once without one; every row counts toward the 50. Values are sealed (AES-256-GCM, key from `CREDENTIALS_SECRET`, purpose `cloud-env-v1`) and are write-only: the console shows names, never values; only the build that needs them receives them, and the build log hides them.
  - **Importing a `.env` file:** `POST /cloud/projects/:id/env/import` `{ content, production, preview, overwrite }` (ADMIN; session or token; `content` up to 256 KB) adds many at once. Dotenv syntax: one `KEY=value` per line, blank lines and `#` comments skipped, an optional `export ` prefix, values unquoted (trimmed; a ` #` starts a comment), in single quotes (as written) or in double quotes (`\n`, `\r`, `\t`, `\"` and `\\` escaped, and may span lines). Names and values follow the rules above. All or nothing, in one transaction: any line that doesn't parse, a bad name or value, a name twice in the file, or going past the plan's variables per project refuses the whole import (`400` with `errors: [{ line, key?, message }]`, never a value); an existing name is replaced only with `overwrite` (otherwise it is skipped). An import only writes general variables (no `branch`): "existing" means a general one with that name, and branch variables are left alone but count toward the limit. Answers `{ created: [keys], updated: [keys], skipped: [keys] }`, never values. One event `env.imported` with the keys and the targets.
  - **Console:** "Import .env" in the project's variables: a textarea (or a file chosen from the computer, read in the browser), the targets and "Replace existing", then the result; errors show their line. **CLI:** `codeqlick env import <file> [--production] [--preview] [--overwrite]` (both targets when neither is given; `--branch` is refused: branch variables are added one by one with `env add`), printing the keys created, updated and skipped.
- **What triggers a build** (the webhook, signature `X-Hub-Signature-256` checked, deliveries deduplicated by `X-GitHub-Delivery` for a day): a push to the production branch (a **production** build); a push to another branch (a **preview**, when on); a pull request opened, reopened or updated from a branch of the same repository (a preview with its number; **pull requests from forks are never built**: their code is a stranger's). Branch deletions, tags and commits whose message contains `[skip cloud]` are ignored. A newer build for the same project and branch cancels the queued ones before it. "Deploy again" in the console builds the branch's latest commit. A deploy hook (a secret URL a CMS can call) does the same for its branch.
- **Scheduled rebuilds:** a connected project can rebuild its production branch on a schedule, for sites that read content when they build (a CMS, an API): `off` (default), `hourly` (at a minute), `daily` (at a time) or `weekly` (a weekday and a time), Madrid time (`rebuildFrequency`, `rebuildMinute` 0–59, `rebuildHour` 0–23, `rebuildWeekday` 1–7, Monday first; the next run in `rebuildNextAt`). At the time the API (every minute, `CloudBuildsService`, off in tests; each run claimed by moving `rebuildNextAt` on first) queues a production build of the branch's latest commit with trigger `SCHEDULE`, exactly like "Deploy again", unless a build of that branch is already queued or running (then that run is skipped). It counts towards the month's builds like any other; one refused for the limit counts as failed, and so does a run whose branch can't be read from GitHub (deleted branch or repository, suspended installation). Suspended projects skip their runs; deleting a project or disconnecting Git switches the schedule off. Five scheduled builds failing in a row (`rebuildFailures`; a successful one resets it, canceled ones don't count) switch the schedule off and email the team's admins once. The **Git** tab sets it and shows the next run; scheduled builds show "scheduled" in the builds list.
- **Build** (`CloudBuild`): `QUEUED` → `RUNNING` → `SUCCEEDED` (with its deployment) or `FAILED` (with the reason), or `CANCELED`. One running build per team at a time; at most 15 minutes; the free plan builds 200 times a month per team (Madrid calendar month; over it builds are refused with a note in the console and a failed commit status). The log (stdout and stderr, at most 1 MB: the end is kept) is shown live in the console and kept with the build 30 days; builds are kept 90 days. **Downloading the log**: any member (session or token, also a read-only one) gets the whole kept log as a file from `GET /cloud/projects/:id/builds/:buildId/log` (`text/plain; charset=utf-8`, `Content-Disposition: attachment; filename="<project-slug>-build-<number>.log"` (plus `filename*=UTF-8''…`), `Cache-Control: no-store`), as stored: at most 1 MB, so for a long build only the end. While the build runs it answers what is there so far. A build that is unknown, of another project, or of a project the caller can't see (not a member, deleted) answers `404` "Build not found", like `GET /builds/:buildId`. The API empties a build's log 30 days after it finished (its daily cleanup sets `log` to `''`), so after that the answer is `200` with an empty file, not `404`. Nothing is recorded in the team's events.
- **Build server** (decision 41): a build host runs `codeqlick-builder` (section 8.4; for now the production VPS itself, with every build sandboxed by gVisor), an agent that **asks** the API for work (`POST /v1/cloud/builder/claim`, every 5 s; it needs no open port). For each build the API hands it a one-hour installation token for that repository only, the commit, the settings and the variables. The agent clones the commit, runs install and build in a **throwaway Docker container** (`node:<version>-bookworm-slim` under gVisor, the agent's own unprivileged user, 2 CPUs at low priority, 2 GB of memory, 512 processes, internet only: no access to the host, its private networks or Docker; removed afterwards), sends the log every 2 seconds and a heartbeat every 30, and finally uploads the output folder as a `tar.gz`, which the API deploys exactly like `codeqlick deploy` (same checks and limits, decision 34), together with the `codeqlick.json` of the build's root folder as `config` when it has one. A running build without a heartbeat for 2 minutes is failed ("the build server stopped answering").
- **Dependency cache** (roadmap item proposed by the agent team, 2026-11-14; decision 68): builds keep the package managers' download stores between runs, so a build doesn't download every dependency again.
  - **Two caches per project.** `production` is used by builds whose `target` is `production` (the project's production branch, `CloudProject.gitBranch`); `preview` by every other build (other branches and pull requests). A build reads and writes only its own cache, so a preview build never writes what production builds install: code from a branch or a pull request can't poison the production cache. Caches are never shared between projects or teams.
  - **What is cached:** the stores only, never `node_modules` or the build output: npm (`npm_config_cache=/cache/npm`, with its logs sent to `/tmp` by `npm_config_logs_dir` so they are not kept), Yarn 1 (`YARN_CACHE_FOLDER=/cache/yarn`), Yarn 2+ (`YARN_GLOBAL_FOLDER=/cache/yarn-berry`) and pnpm (`npm_config_store_dir` and `pnpm_config_store_dir`, both `/cache/pnpm`). These are set with `-e`, which Docker applies over the project's own variables (`--env-file`), so a project can't point the cache elsewhere. They are content-addressed, so a changed lockfile still reuses every package that matches, and the cache's key doesn't depend on the lockfile. The cache is also per Node major version (a store built under one Node may not suit another).
  - **Key:** the API computes it at claim time as `<projectId>/<production|preview>-node<major>` (`major` is the project's `nodeVersion`: `20`, `22` or `24`), for example `3f9c…/production-node22`. It matches `[a-z0-9-]+/[a-z0-9-]+`.
  - **Without cache:** `POST /cloud/projects/:id/builds` accepts `noCache` (`true`). The build is stored with `noCache: true` and the console shows it. The claim then carries `cache: { key, fresh: true }`: the agent deletes the stored cache before the install (`Dependency cache: cleared for this build`), so the build starts empty and its fresh cache replaces the old one for the builds after it. Builds started by a push or a pull request always use the cache. The console's "New build" form has a "Without cache" checkbox and a failed build's page offers "Retry without cache" (a new build of the branch's latest commit, like "Deploy again", with `noCache`); the CLI has `codeqlick builds start --no-cache`. It is the way out of a cache that has gone bad.
  - **Log:** the agent writes `Dependency cache: restored (<size>)` or `Dependency cache: empty` before the install, or `Dependency cache: off for this build` when the claim has no cache; after the build, `Dependency cache: saved (<size>)`. With `fresh` it says `Dependency cache: cleared for this build`. When the limits below delete the cache it says so (`Dependency cache: over the project's limit (<size>), deleted; the next build starts empty`).
  - **Limits** (the agent's, section 8.4; the same for FREE and PRO, no plan difference): after each build that used a cache the agent measures it; above `BUILDER_CACHE_PROJECT_MB` (default 1024) it deletes that cache. Then, while the whole cache folder is above `BUILDER_CACHE_TOTAL_MB` (default 10240), it deletes the least recently used caches (last use is the folder's modification time, set at each use). Builds run one at a time on an agent, so nothing is locked. The agent measures only after the build's container is gone (a cancelled or timed-out build is removed with `docker rm -f` and waited for; its cache is then left as it is, not measured or saved), so build code can't swap files under it. Before measuring or deleting it makes the whole tree readable and writable for its user (`chmod -R u+rwX`, which doesn't follow symbolic links), counts the space really used (allocated blocks, at least 4 KB per file or folder, so millions of empty files count), never follows symbolic links, and deletes a cache it can't fully read. The least-recently-used pass runs after the output is uploaded, so it doesn't delay deployments, and only looks at folders whose names match the key pattern. Deleting a project deletes no cache at once: its caches go out by that same least-recently-used rule.
  - **Disk guard:** the API knows the disk's state at claim time (`CloudDiskService`, "Disk guard (one server)" below). While it is `full` builds still run, but the claim sends `cache: null` and the log says `Dependency cache: off for this build`, so nothing grows on the disk. The API's reading is that of the disk holding `CLOUD_ROOT`: it is the builder's disk only while the builder runs on the same machine; on a separate build server the size limits are what protect its disk.
- **Package managers** (roadmap item proposed by the agent team, 2026-11-14; decision 73): pnpm and Yarn projects build with no setup.
  - **Corepack in every build:** the container's command starts with `mkdir -p /tmp/.corepack-bin && corepack enable --install-directory /tmp/.corepack-bin` (Corepack needs the folder to exist), followed by `;` rather than `&&`, and puts that folder first on `PATH`, with `COREPACK_ENABLE_DOWNLOAD_PROMPT=0` (the container's user can't write to `/usr/local/bin`, so a plain `corepack enable` fails). So `pnpm …` and `yarn …` work in any install or build command, custom ones included. Corepack fetches the version named by the project's `packageManager` field, or its own default. The Node 20, 22 and 24 images ship Corepack; when it is missing the build goes on without it.
  - **Automatic choice:** each of the two commands is replaced only while it is still its default (install `npm ci`, build `npm run build`); a command the team typed is never changed. A valid `packageManager` field in the root folder's `package.json` (`pnpm@…`, `yarn@…`, `npm@…`) names the manager, whatever lockfiles there are (Corepack refuses to run any other one). Without it, the lockfile does: the agent looks in the root folder, then in each parent up to the repository root, never above it (monorepos keep it at the top); the first folder with any lockfile wins, and inside a folder the order is `pnpm-lock.yaml` → pnpm, `yarn.lock` → Yarn, `package-lock.json` or `npm-shrinkwrap.json` → npm; with no lockfile, npm. "With a lockfile" below means that the chosen manager's own lockfile is in that nearest folder with any lockfile.
