Spots are pre-moderated because a wrong or dangerous place, or one near children, harms people and the directory's trust; reviews are post-moderated, with reports hiding minors and illegal content at once. OpenStreetMap tiles are free with attribution; a tile provider can replace them if traffic grows (OSM's tile usage policy). No GPS of a person is ever stored or sent: the map is centred in the browser and a check-in is only a spot. Age is declared, as in decision 27; real age assurance can be added. Split into two pull requests: the directory, then the social network |
| 79 | Cruising moderation: an anonymous removal request hides a spot at once only for MINOR or ILLEGAL, and a spot removed by moderation (REMOVED) is final for its proposer | Owner's decision (2026-11-15) after the review of phase 1: hiding on any reason would let anyone empty the directory from an anonymous form, and letting a removed spot go back to review reopened what the team had decided. A rejection in the normal review (REJECTED) can still be corrected and sent again |
| 80 | A project's protection can be bypassed by automation with one separate secret, sent in the `X-Codeqlick-Bypass` header, stored as a SHA-256 hash, independent of the password | CI end-to-end tests and link checkers can't type a password or keep a cookie, and sharing the password with them means rotating it everywhere when it changes. A second credential per project (as Vercel's Protection Bypass for Automation and Netlify's site-protection tokens) lets the password change without breaking the pipeline and the pipeline's secret be revoked without logging visitors out. It is a header and never a query parameter, because URLs reach logs, history and `Referer`; no cookie is set, so a leaked token isn't extended by a session. It is hashed like API tokens (`cqk_`, SHA-256) because a random 256-bit secret needs no slow hash, shown once and replaced by generating again. It rides the existing single `auth_request` and its 60 s cache (decision 50, 52) by adding the header's value to the cache key, so a bypass answer is never served to a request without it and the unprotected fast path is untouched. Wrong tokens have no per-IP limit (a random 256-bit token can't be guessed and comparing one is a cheap SHA-256 plus a constant-time compare; Nginx's 60 s cache and `limit_req` bound the load), so nothing about a visitor is stored and strangers or neighbours on a shared IP can't lock automation out. Monitors and preview checks don't send it: they stay what a visitor would see |
| 81 | Production gets a weekly website check at a slot fixed by the project, and the admins are told when the score drops | Quality slips after releases and nobody looks: preview checks (decision 69) catch a pull request, not a content or config change that lands on production. Weekly is enough to notice a slow slide and cheap (one audit a week per project). A deterministic slot from the project's id spreads the load without a scheduler setting, and a claim by moving `productionCheckNextAt` (decision 65) means one run per slot with several API processes. It is a project setting, off by default, counted in `auditsPerHour` and skipped past it, like previews; and skipped for password-protected production, where the check would score the password page. The hosting address, not a custom domain, is checked so weeks compare like with like. A drop is 10 points overall or in a category, or a pass turning to fail, ignoring the checks that vary between runs (`ttfb`, the two PageSpeed ones); the next week compares with the previous one, so a steady low score alerts once. Scheduled audits are kept 90 days (the history needs 12 weeks) instead of copying a summary onto the project. Alerts reuse the admins' email and the channels' `monitorAlerts` switch (decision 64) rather than adding a switch |
| 82 | No AI attribution anywhere in the repository: commits use the owner's identity and carry no assistant trailers, and pull requests no generated-by footers; a CI check enforces it | The repository is public and its history speaks for the studio. Project settings (`.claude/settings.json`) turn the footers off, but sessions in the cloud commit under their own git identity, so only a check on every pull request catches it before it reaches `main` |
| 83 | Cruising nicknames can be changed whenever the member wants, like on Instagram, with no limit; the old one stays reserved for its owner for 14 days | Owner's request (2026-11-15): people expect to rename themselves without waiting. Reserving the old name for 14 days keeps someone else from posing as the person right after a change (the reason they were fixed at first), and lets the owner go back. Everything else is tied to the account, so a rename breaks no conversation, block or report |
| 84 | The team seeds Cruising's directory itself: one spot or a CSV/GeoJSON import (≤ 500 rows) published at once, with a dry run and duplicates within 100 m skipped | Production had no LIVE spot: a directory that is empty on day one keeps nobody, and waiting for members to propose every place (each one reviewed) would take months. Team spots skip the queue because the team is the reviewer; no proposer, so `createdById` is null |
| 85 | Cruising members save favourite spots and are alerted by Web Push (or a daily email they opt into) when someone checks in at one, never told who; at most one alert per spot every 3 hours and 10 a day | A check-in is only useful if the people who'd come learn about it in time. Saying "someone" and never the spot on the lock screen keeps the app's rule that nothing outside it gives anything away; the caps keep a busy spot from turning into spam |
| 86 | Cruising search is by text (cities and spot names), and "active now" counts are shown only to entered members, on the map's 0.25° grid | People look for a city, not coordinates. Presence is personal data about the sex lives of the people present, so it stays inside the members' area; the grid means no request reveals where the member is looking from |
| 87 | Cruising profiles carry photos and videos of the member, explicit content allowed for entered members, published at once and reviewed afterwards; verifying the person is optional and gives a badge, and reports of a minor, no consent, stolen or illegal hide an item at once | Owner's choice (2026-11-16): the app is for adults and its members expect it, and waiting for review would slow it down. What keeps it lawful is consent at every upload, hiding at once on the serious reports with the team emailed, a queue so every new item is still seen by a moderator, blocked hashes so removed images can't come back, and media never leaving the members' area |
| 88 | Cruising meetups: a member plans a time at a LIVE spot, others say they're going (or anonymously), and those who saved the spot are alerted | An empty spot becomes a concrete plan; it reuses favourites' alerts and stays inside the members' area |
| 89 | A spot's busiest hours come from check-ins as decaying hourly counters in the spot's local time, shown to members after 10 check-ins | Answers "when is there anyone?" without keeping who or when: only counters, and only once there are enough to say nothing about one person |
| 90 | Traveller mode: a member announces a city and dates (≤ 14 days, ≤ 60 days ahead), listed to that city's members unless kept private | People cruise most when travelling; meeting locals before arriving is what they look for, and it's opt-in per trip |
| 91 | Safety alerts on spots (police, theft, aggression, surveillance, closed) that expire, are confirmed or ended by other members, and never show who posted them | The information members need most to stay safe is fresh and local; expiry and confirmations keep it true, anonymity keeps posters safe |
| 92 | Spots carry structured tags, opening hours and tips, filterable on the map and shown on the public page | Members filter for what matters to them, and the public pages say more to search engines than a description |
| 93 | Helpful votes on reviews and contributor badges (Explorer, Guide, Helpful, Local) from counters | Rewards the people who build the directory, with no money involved |
| 94 | Sexual health fields (PrEP, last test month) are opt-in with their own explicit consent and private by default; reminders; a team-curated public map of testing centres | Health data is the most sensitive there is: nothing unless the member asks for it, and the useful public part (where to get tested) needs no personal data |
| 95 | "Check on me": up to 2 trusted contacts who accepted by email are emailed when a member's timer runs out, with only what the member chose to share; email only for now (SMS needs paid credits) | Meeting strangers in remote places has real risks; a timer someone outside the app can act on is the simplest safety net, and the contact's consent and the member's choices keep it from exposing anyone |
| 96 | Cruising is an installable web app (manifest, icons, offline page via its service worker) | It's used on phones, and an icon on the home screen that opens the map full screen is what an app store would give, without one |
| 97 | Cruising winks (one per pair a day) and reciprocal profile visits, off by default | A wink is interest with no effort; visits engage but expose browsing, so only those who opt in are recorded and see others (privacy by default) |
| 98 | A notification centre in the Cruising app with everything that happened, kept 30 days | Push and email are optional; the centre is where nothing is missed, with no extra exposure outside the app |
| 99 | A private album shared per person from a conversation and revocable at any time | Members want to show more to someone they talk to, not to everyone; a grant per person, revoked by a block, keeps control with the owner |
| 100 | One moderated chat room per city, no contact details or links ever, accounts over 24 hours, messages kept 7 days | Gives life to cities with few spots; the limits keep spam and outing risks low |
| 101 | Admin statistics for Cruising: weekly aggregates for 12 weeks and top places, no person listed | The owner needs to see what works; aggregates answer that without another view of members' data |
| 102 | A skippable 4-step onboarding with suggested spots | Most newcomers leave before trying anything; a short guided start gets them to favourites and notifications |
| 103 | Cruising also in French, German, Italian and Portuguese; the main site stays in English and Spanish; machine-made translations to be reviewed | The directory is worldwide and each language multiplies what search engines index; limiting it to Cruising keeps the rest of the platform unchanged |
| 104 | Passkeys (WebAuthn) for every account, on `codeqlick.com` as relying party so one passkey works on every host; a passkey sign-in counts as both factors, but admins still need an authenticator app | Phishing-proof and faster than password plus code for clients, admins and app members alike; keeping TOTP mandatory for admins avoids weakening the rule that protects the panel |
| 105 | Status page email subscriptions are double opt-in rows of their own, fed by an outbox of notices and sent in batches, capped per plan and per hour; Codeqlick is the team's processor and the Cloud terms move to `2026-11-17`, accepted again in the console | Owner's roadmap item and choice (2026-11-17) to update the legal texts with it. Notices are the team's own words, so only they are emailed, never raw monitor flaps. Own table because subscribers belong to a team (the controller), not to an account; double opt-in is the consent proof and, with the per-address daily limit, stops the feature being used to pester a third party. Turnstile is not added because the newsletter and the other public forms use the honeypot and the 3-second rule. An outbox row written with the notice, plus a batch cursor claimed before sending, gives at most one email per subscriber and survives restarts and two API processes (a crash loses a batch rather than doubling it, as decision 67); the per-team hourly counter and plan cap protect the shared SMTP account and the sender's reputation, and 24-hour-old dispatches are dropped. Maintenance start has no event of its own, so a minute loop claims it with `startNotifiedAt`. One-click unsubscribe follows decision 70. No IP is kept, a team's deletion cascades, and a downgrade only stops new sign-ups so nobody loses what they signed up for. Re-accepting the terms is asked in the console only: blocking API tokens would break deploys from CI over a clause that only adds protections |
| 106 | A basic website can be in Spanish and English: the client writes the English twins of the texts in the editor, English pages live under `/en` on the same domain, and an empty English text shows the Spanish one | Owner's choices (2026-11-17, CQP-99): tourist-area businesses need English, and it is included in every basic website. The client knows their business and how they want to say it, and machine translation would put words nobody checked on their site, so we only add the fields. Keeping the twins in the site content means they follow the draft, publishing, revisions and discarding like any text; mirroring the content's paths keeps one template and one schema to maintain and lets the editor reuse its fields, and items line up by position so lists stay in step. Falling back to Spanish keeps the English pages whole while the client translates, and counting what is missing tells them what is left without blocking publishing. The same addresses under `/en` with `hreflang` (`x-default` Spanish) is what search engines expect of one site in two languages, needs no second domain or certificate, and lets the switcher find the twin by the prefix. Builds of sites with it off get nothing new, so they stay as they were |
| 107 | One header and one menu drawer for every web app: logo, wordmark, the app's call to action and bell, and a ☰ button opening a native `<dialog>` from the right with the app's links in grouped sections, the other apps, the language and the account | Owner's request and choice (2026-11-17): the apps' headers had grown to a dozen flat links (Cruising) that wrapped into several rows on phones. A hamburger for all apps, on every screen size, keeps one pattern to learn and to maintain. A native modal `<dialog>` gives the focus trap, the inert page and Escape without our own code and without roles oxlint's accessibility rules forbid; the configuration as pure per-app builders keeps today's visibility rules testable, and the labels stay on the server so no new client messages ship |
| 109 | A strict Content Security Policy (a nonce per response with `'strict-dynamic'`, no `'unsafe-inline'` scripts) on every host the website serves, report-only first behind `CSP_ENFORCE`; the same HSTS (with `includeSubDomains`, without `preload`), `Permissions-Policy`, `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy` and frame rules from one module; reports kept as counted `CSP` groups with a sample and no IP; every public mutation with its own rate limit, checked by a test; the mail domain's SPF, DKIM and DMARC checked by a script | Owner's request (2026-11-17). `'unsafe-inline'` let any injected `<script>` run, which is what a CSP is for; a nonce is the only strict option Next.js applies to its own inline bootstrap, and its cost (pages render per request) is CPU on our one server, not API load, since public data stays in the data cache. Report-only first because a broken policy fails silently in visitors' browsers (Turnstile, ads, WebRTC, Stripe, passkeys) and our tests can't reach every third party; the browser tests run it enforced, so a regression in our own code fails CI. Reports reuse the errors log (one place to look) but never email and have their own cap, because extensions and ad networks make noise; the origin and the directive are enough to act on, and no IP is needed for that. `includeSubDomains` is safe because every browsable name under codeqlick.com is HTTPS; `preload` is left to the owner since leaving the list takes months. The test makes a forgotten `@Throttle` on a new public form fail CI instead of relying on the global 100 a minute. The DNS script only reads and prints: the zone is changed by the owner in OVH |
| 110 | An optional maintenance plan for managed sites, 19 € a month + IVA, as a Stripe subscription per project, with 3 included change requests a month counted on the support requests, and a free "included" mark for the team | Owner's request and price (2026-11-17). Clients want someone to look after their site and make small changes; a subscription gives the studio recurring revenue without changing what a purchase already includes (decision 20 promises hosting and fixes with no time limit, so the plan adds on top and nothing is taken from anyone who paid). Stripe Checkout, its customer portal and its invoices are reused as for Cloud's PRO plan (decision 46): no card data or invoicing of our own, and the state is re-read from the subscription on every event. The webhook gives each event to the first handler that claims it by the metadata `kind`, so one endpoint and one set of events serve the three kinds of payment. Counting change requests (not hours) keeps the allowance automatic, visible to the client as a counter and enforceable without the team logging time; marking each request in or out of the plan when it is opened tells the team at once what to quote. "Included" is a column with its reason, not a fake subscription, so it never collides with Stripe and leaves a trace of why. The price lives in Stripe and the plan stays hidden until it is set there: no live object is made by code |
| 111 | Public read buckets through Garage's website endpoint under `s3.<domain>/_public/<bucket>/`, sandboxed, with CORS rules set by Garage's admin API; preview comments in a Codeqlick page on the preview's own host (`/__codeqlick/comments`, the site in a same-origin frame) opened by members from the console with a 2-hour token bound to that deployment's address; the CLI gains `db` and `buckets` | Owner's request (2026-11-17). Garage 2.5 serves public buckets itself (website access) and takes CORS rules in `UpdateBucket`, so no key with owner rights or S3 signing is needed. A path on the existing S3 host needs no new DNS record, certificate or reserved slug, and `_` can't start a bucket name, so it never clashes with path-style S3; `sandbox` keeps an uploaded HTML file from running scripts on a host all public buckets share. For comments, injecting a script into served pages (Nginx `sub_filter`) would touch every visitor's page, break on the site's own CSP and compressed answers, and could leak onto production; a page of our own under the path Nginx already sends to the API changes neither the site nor Nginx, runs only when a member opens it, and can refuse production by Nginx's own `$cq_kind`. The same origin is what lets it follow the frame's path and scroll without code in the site. A header token instead of a cookie needs no cookie on the client's host and can't be forged across sites; binding it to one address and 2 hours limits what a leaked one does. The CLI's confirmations refuse without a terminal unless `--yes`, so CI never hangs or deletes by accident, and `db url` says it resets the password because the API never keeps one |
| 112 | The three 18+ apps share blocks: a block in one is written into the three block tables (with its side effects) and lifted in the three; the per-app tables stay | Owner's request and choice (2026-11-17). A person blocked for harassment in Swipe shouldn't reach the same member in Cruising or Videochat; writing the three tables keeps every existing check and query as it is, instead of rewriting them over a new table |
| 113 | One verification per account (`AdultVerification`), sent from any adult app with "Codeqlick" on the paper, decided in one queue, giving the badge in the three; the approved photo is kept privately in all three apps while the verification stands (deleted when withdrawn or lost, replaced, or the account deleted) | Owner's choice (2026-11-17). Asking the same person three times for the same photo is friction with no safety gain; keeping the photo lets moderators compare uploads and reports with it in any app |
| 114 | Suspensions for MINOR, NON_CONSENSUAL or ILLEGAL decided by the team apply in the three apps with the same end; automatic ones and other reasons stay per app; moderators see a person's history across the three | Owner's choice (2026-11-17). The gravest abuse must not move to a sister app; requiring a moderator's decision before sharing stops one false report from shutting someone out of three apps, and the shared history lets moderators see a pattern |
| 115 | One inbox (Cruising conversations, Swipe chats, calls) and one notification centre (`AdultNotification`, generalising decision 98) inside each adult app; one service worker per host, pushes go to the app's own devices first and to the other hosts only when a device opted in | Owner's choice (2026-11-17). Members of several apps miss messages scattered across three places; keeping the chats on their hosts and merging only the lists avoids moving code, and the opt-in per device stops one app's alerts appearing in another's unasked |
| 116 | Video calls from a Cruising conversation or a Swipe match run on Videochat's host, socket and coturn, and need both people entered in Videochat; they ring 60 s, end when either leaves and nothing is recorded | Owner's choice (2026-11-17). Videochat's host is the only adult one allowed camera and microphone and already has the WebRTC room, reports and blocks; its rules are the ones written for live video, so asking for its entry keeps the 18+ and no-recording terms accepted by both |
| 117 | Linked profiles are opt-in with their own explicit consent: the member chooses the apps; the `@username` becomes the Cruising nickname too (decision 83's hold for both); one photo from a linked app; "Also on" links are reciprocal: shown only to viewers who linked too, for apps both linked and the viewer entered; unlinking is immediate | Owner's choice (2026-11-17). Showing that profiles in sex-related apps belong to one person is a new purpose with special category data, so it needs consent of its own; the username already is Swipe's and Videochat's name; reciprocity makes linking an exchange, and limiting links to shared apps discloses nothing to people who couldn't use them |
| 118 | Swipe and Videochat reach Cruising's level in a separate last phase: installable app with an offline page, the badge everywhere, French, German, Italian and Portuguese | Owner's request (2026-11-17). Same reasons as decisions 96 and 103; done last so the translations cover the new shared texts |
| 119 | Cross-app safety rests on legitimate interest (art. 6.1.f) and the explicit consent at entry, which now names it (art. 9.2.a); linked profiles on a separate consent (art. 6.1.a and 9.2.a); the three terms versions move | Safety sharing is what members expect and must not be optional for someone who could abuse it; linking is a choice, so it is consented to apart and can be withdrawn at any time. A lawyer should confirm tying cross-app safety to entry for art. 9 data |
| 120 | Deleting an account with an active MINOR, NON_CONSENSUAL or ILLEGAL shared suspension keeps an HMAC-SHA256 of its normalised email for the suspension's length (2 years if permanent), and entering any adult app with a matching email is refused | Owner's decision (2026-11-17). Without it, deleting the account and registering again undoes the gravest suspensions; a keyed hash with an end date keeps nothing readable (legitimate interest in safety, art. 6.1.f) |
| 121 | Local service pages: four services × ten Spanish cities, each with copy written for the city, no address, reviews or case studies; one path scheme, statically listed | Owner's request (2026-11-17). "Service + city" searches are how local businesses look for a supplier, and a page per pair is what ranks for them; search engines treat pages that only swap the name as doorway pages, so each city gets its own context and per-service sentence, checked for duplicates by a test. Codeqlick works remotely, so claiming a local address would be false; `ProfessionalService` with `areaServed` says where it works without one |
| 122 | One admin overview of the whole platform (people, revenue, activity, health), 12 Madrid weeks of aggregates computed in SQL and cached 10 minutes; MRR from amounts stored by Stripe's webhook, not from live Stripe calls | Owner's request (2026-11-17). The figures were spread over each app's admin and Stripe's dashboard. Reading Stripe on every visit would tie the page to Stripe's availability and rate limits, and the prices were only known in memory, so the subscription's monthly amount before VAT is kept on the row the webhook already writes (one nullable column on `cloud_teams` and on `client_projects`); the maintenance plans paid before are filled with decision 110's 1 900 cents, PRO teams fill in at their next renewal and are shown as unpriced until then. Aggregates only, as decision 101, so no person's data leaves its app's admin |
